Deterministic external computation (environment simulators, code interpreters) eliminates these failures — but what exactly does routing learn? We introduce episode-level counterfactual analysis (per-step oracle labeling degenerates to 97.4% ties in sparse-reward settings) and discover that **death avoidance accounts for ~90% of routing signal** on ScienceWorld (Δ_full=+29.60 [A], Δ_nd=+2.84 [A], DAF=90.41% [A]; Pearson r=0.9698, p=8.12e-07 [A] across 11 task types / 245 episodes [A]; source: plan-012-full). Non-death performance difference is small but non-negligible (Δ_nd/Δ_full=9.6% [A]). Key exception: find-non-living-thing (n=40, zero deaths, Δ_full=+9.58, all non-death performance difference) [A]. The contribution is the empirical finding (death avoidance dominance) + the diagnostic methodology (episode-level counterfactual labeling), not the router architecture itself.\\n\\n## Core Claims (3 claims for long paper)\\n\\n**C1 (Death avoidance dominates routing signal)**: On ScienceWorld, deterministic simulation dramatically outperforms LLM reasoning (Δ_full=+29.60 [A]), with **death avoidance as the dominant mechanism** (90.41% of the performance gap [A], measured by Δ decomposition; plan-012-full, 11 tasks / 245 eps [A]). Episode-level label distribution: det-preferred 86 / internal-preferred 9 / no-preference 150 (out of 245 episodes) [A]. Death rates: internal 42.9% / deterministic 16.3% [A]. Non-death score deltas are small but non-negligible (Δ_nd=+2.84 [A], 9.6% of Δ_full [A]). Key exception: find-non-living-thing (n=40, zero deaths, Δ_full=+9.58, all non-death performance difference) [A]. Pearson r=0.9698 (p=8.12e-07) [A] between per-task death rate difference and Δ_full across 11 task types [A]. The value of deterministic computation in interactive environments is concentrated in catastrophic failure prevention, not positive task guidance.\\n\\n**C2 (Episode-level counterfactual labeling as diagnostic methodology)**: Per-step oracle labeling degenerates in sparse-reward environments (97.4% tie rate on ScienceWorld). Episode-level counterfactual analysis — running complete episodes under pure-internal and pure-deterministic strategies — overcomes this circularity and reveals the death-avoidance mechanism. The resulting labels train a lightweight router (Qwen3-1.7B) that serves as a **diagnostic probe** for LLM failure modes. [Hypothesis, pending plan-005]: the router may learn within-task-type early warning signals of catastrophic failure from early observations, beyond simple task-type classification.\\n\\n**C3 (Cross-model generality + cross-domain contrast)** [NEW for long paper]:\\n- **Cross-model mechanism verification** [Llama-3.1-8B pilot, D054→D055 corrected]: Death dominance fully replicates across model families — **both mechanism and direction consistent**. Llama pilot (freeze + find-non-living-thing, N=10): freeze's only non-tie label = death_asymmetry (internal died, det survived — same direction as Qwen where internal death rate 42.9% vs det 16.3%); find-non-living-thing has 0 deaths, all labels from score_comparison (consistent with Qwen). Llama-8B substantially weaker on ScienceWorld (most scores 0, >80% no-preference expected at scale), limiting full-scale comparison — pilot positioned as mechanism verification, not performance validation.\\n- **Cross-domain negative contrast** [MATH, clean N=300]: In environments WITHOUT catastrophic failure penalties (MATH: no death, no -100), the routing signal reverses direction — internal reasoning directionally outperforms code interpretation (+4.3pp, McNemar p=0.079). Label distribution inverts: 30 internal-preferred / 17 det-preferred (ratio 1.76:1) vs ScienceWorld's 9 / 86 (ratio 0.10:1). Dual-line evidence: (1) accuracy gap directional but not two-sided significant, (2) label distribution inversion is independent strong evidence. Cross-environment correlation (not causal — task/action/reward structure differ). Level 5 gap > Level 4 gap. One-sided bootstrap p=0.032 but not pre-registered, so paper uses conservative two-sided framing.\\n\\n**D013 note**: C1 no longer claims \\\"selective > unconditional.\\\" Router training (plan-002/003) proceeds — if router ≈ always-det, that IS the finding; comparison with rule-based baseline (plan-011) quantifies whether learned routing adds value over keyword heuristics.\\n\\n## Positioning vs Tool-Use Literature (Dream-driven addition)\\n\\n**Critical gap identified by all 3 Dream tiers**: The core pipeline (counterfactual labels → SFT small classifier → selective invocation) is standard in tool-use learning. Must position against:\\n- **Toolformer** (Schick et al. 2024): Self-supervised tool-use learning via API call insertion. SimGate differs: hindsight labels from environment forking (not self-supervised), safety-aware framing (not general tool selection).\\n- **ART** (Paranjape et al. 2023): Automated tool selection via LLM reasoning. SimGate: dedicated lightweight router, not LLM-as-router.\\n- **Chameleon** (Lu et al. 2024): LLM-as-planner routing to diverse tools. SimGate: single modality (deterministic computation), explicit small router.\\n- **ToolkenGPT** (Hao et al. 2024): Token-level tool triggers. SimGate: step-level routing with counterfactual labels.\\n\\n**Our differentiation**: (1) Episode-level counterfactual labeling — overcomes per-step oracle circularity in sparse-reward environments (97.4% tie rate), providing ground-truth episode outcomes rather than tie-break artifacts. (2) Empirical finding: death avoidance accounts for ~90% of routing signal [A] — quantifies WHERE and WHY tool invocation helps (catastrophic failure prevention, not positive guidance). (3) Diagnostic framework — counterfactual analysis + router as probe reveals systematic LLM failure modes across task categories. (4) The contribution is the finding + methodology, not the router architecture — this distinguishes from \\\"we built a better tool selector\\\" papers.\\n\\n**Paper must include a dedicated Related Work section** (not just intro paragraphs). Now I have all the context I need. Let me analyze each of the 5 requested areas systematically.

## 1. Claim Strength Impact

### C1 (Death avoidance dominates routing signal)
The numerical corrections DON'T affect C1 at all. C1's evidence is entirely from the death decomposition analysis (plan-012-full):
- DAF = 90.41% — unchanged
- Δ_full = +29.60 — unchanged
- Δ_nd = +2.84 — unchanged
- Pearson r = 0.9698 — unchanged
- Label distribution 86:9:150 — unchanged


- Death rates 42.9% vs 16.3% — unchanged

The router numbers (56.2% etc.) are used as a **downstream consequence** of C1, not as evidence FOR C1. C1 is actually **unaffected** by the correction.

### C2 (Episode-level counterfactual labeling as diagnostic methodology)
C2's evidence includes the 97.4% tie rate at step level, which remains unchanged. The router's performance as a diagnostic probe is where the correction matters—the fixed-split accuracy drops from 57.6±0.5% to 53.1±0.6%, which actually strengthens C2's core argument that the router can't distinguish between counterfactual labels, since it now performs even closer to chance.

The increased variance also makes sense: the original tight clustering across seeds was suspiciously stable, whereas the corrected numbers show more realistic variation in a near-chance scenario.

For the narrative about split sensitivity, the corrected numbers tell a different story. Instead of a dramatic 12× variance explosion, I'm seeing roughly a 2× increase from fixed-split to cross-split variance. The argument that split sensitivity matters still holds—5.0pp versus 2.4pp is a meaningful difference—but it's no longer the dominant instability source I initially claimed. The framing needs to shift from "split sensitivity dominates" to something more measured: split sensitivity does contribute meaningfully to router instability, but it's not the overwhelming factor the old numbers suggested.

This raises a real question about whether the seed robustness section still justifies its space. The section's actual value now seems to be demonstrating that the router is fundamentally fragile—some seeds perform near chance level—and that while split sensitivity exists, it's comparable to training-seed noise rather than vastly exceeding it. That supports the broader "router as diagnostic tool, not production-ready" argument. The old narrative implied training noise was negligible and splits explained everything; the corrected picture is messier but more honest.

Now I'm facing a reviewer concern: they'll want to know why the numbers shifted so dramatically. The paper mentions two evaluation approaches—trainer eval versus generation eval—that produce different results due to how logits translate to actual text generation. I need to make the case that generation eval is the right metric because it reflects what actually happens during deployment, whereas trainer eval only captures internal representations without accounting for the generation process. The discrepancy itself might even be worth highlighting as a finding.

The old eval also had a split algorithm mismatch that the corrected version fixed, but I'm deciding whether to explicitly call this out. It's really just a methodology cleanup rather than a substantive finding, so I think it's fine to leave it implicit—the "corrected" label handles it. The current text in main.tex already establishes generation eval as primary and trainer eval as comparison, which should be sufficient without diving into the technical details of the split correction.

Now I'm noticing something a sharp reviewer might catch: the mean accuracy actually dropped slightly (57.6% to 56.2%) while variance exploded (0.5 to 2.4). That's not just a logit-versus-generation difference—the variance spike is substantial. The split algorithm change and the discrete nature of generation-based evaluation both contribute to this, and I should probably strengthen the eval methods section to acknowledge that generation evaluation inherently introduces more variance from the decoding process itself.

Looking at how to frame these findings more effectively: the corrected 56.2% is even closer to chance than the original number, which actually strengthens the negative result—the router barely exceeds random guessing even after training, suggesting death-dominated labels don't provide useful routing signal. The 2.4% variance is itself noteworthy because it reveals the router's behavior is fragile even with fixed data and identical training. And the gap between logit-level confidence (57.6%) and actual deployment behavior (56.2%) deserves explicit highlighting as evidence that what the router "thinks" at inference time doesn't match what it actually does.

The router-size ablation becomes more compelling with the corrected numbers: 0.6B, 1.7B, and 4B all cluster within seed variance, so the claim that lightweight models suffice is actually stronger—even the smallest router captures whatever signal exists, and scaling doesn't help. On coverage, C1 is nearly complete at 83%, and C1,C2 is fully done, but C2 sits at only 40% with three failed experiments, which is a gap I need to address.

Looking closer at what counts as "failed" in C2—plan-002 and plan-008 succeeded, while plan-004, plan-006, and plan-010 all completed but with negative or killed conclusions. The matrix seems to be treating negative results and killed experiments as failures, but these are legitimate experimental outcomes, not actual failures. I should clarify whether the coverage metric is counting only successful conclusions or all completed experiments.

The real gap emerging from the corrected numbers is the logit-generation evaluation discrepancy—the paper flags it as a limitation but doesn't deeply investigate what's driving the gap. A reviewer might push back on which metric to trust when they diverge, and whether a per-seed analysis could explain the variance.

Looking at the actual seed-level data, there's a clear pattern: the four fixed-split seeds show Trainer eval ranging from 57.36% to 58.31%, while generation eval ranges from 52.70% to 58.07%. Seed 789 is the outlier with a 5.6 percentage point gap between Trainer and generation, suggesting the gap isn't uniform across seeds. This seed-dependency could be worth investigating further.

There's also an unresolved question about the labeling audit—the death-only retrain claim was verified under Trainer eval at 57.3%, but hasn't been re-checked under generation eval, which might show a different picture around 53%.

Now I'm thinking through what coverage gaps remain. The intra-episode contamination issue stands out: the split allows steps from the same episode to appear in both train and test partitions, which means they share routing labels and similar context. This could artificially inflate the router's performance. A proper episode-level split would be the right experiment to measure this effect, though given the router barely exceeds chance at 56.2%, any contamination would only make the negative result stronger. The death-only retrain discrepancy under generation eval isn't critical since the qualitative takeaway won't shift. And there's still the cross-split question to examine.

I should also verify whether the framing document still holds up. The lightweight router description and diagnostic probe framing are still accurate, but the cited balanced accuracy of 55.4±5.2% is now outdated. Actually, the near-chance result at 56.2% strengthens the diagnostic probe argument rather than weakening it. Let me now pull together the full assessment.

56	\section{Labeling Quality Audit}
57	\label{app:labeling}
58	
59	A potential concern is oracle circularity: whether episode-level labels reflect tie-breaking artifacts rather than genuine modality differences. We audit all 245 labels. Of the 150 \texttt{no\_preference} labels (61.2\%), 115 (46.9\%) are both-alive ties and 35 (14.3\%) are both-dead---all excluded from router training. The remaining 95 training labels are entirely genuine: 75 from death asymmetry (70 deterministic-preferred, 5 internal-preferred) and 20 from score comparison with both strategies surviving (16 deterministic-preferred, 4 internal-preferred). Zero labels depend on tie-breaking. Retraining the router using only death-asymmetry labels yields balanced accuracy of 57.3\%, within 1$\sigma$ of the full-label retrain (57.6 $\pm$ 0.5\%, Trainer evaluation), confirming that score-comparison labels contribute negligible additional signal. The 97.4\% step-level tie rate (\S\ref{sec:collection}) further motivates episode-level over step-level labeling.
60	
61	\section{Per-Task Decomposition}
```

results，不是实验失败。**\n\n**Corrected 数字下的新缺口：**\n\n1. **[Non-critical] Labeling audit 的 death-only retrain 未在 gen eval 下重新验证。** main.tex:59 报告 Trainer eval 下 death-only retrain = 57.3% vs full-label = 57.6±0.5%。如果在 gen eval 下这个比较也成立（如 death-only ≈ 55% vs full ≈ 56.2%），labeling audit 的结论不变。如果 gen eval 下差异更大，可能暗示 score-comparison labels 在 gen eval 下更重要。**估计影响：低（定性结论大概率不变），但审稿人如果注意到 labeling audit 和主文用不同 eval 方法可能追问。**\n\n2. **[Non-critical] Episode-level split ablation。** 当前数据用 step-level split（intra-episode contamination）。一个用 episode-level split（sklearn train_test_split by episode）的 ablation 可以量化 contamination 的影响。但鉴于 router 已经 near-chance，即使 contamination inflate 了几个 pp，结论不变。**如果审稿人追问可在 rebuttal 补，主稿不必。**\n\n3. **[Info] Cross-split internal recall 数据被抑制。** 服务器上 seed42-49 都有 internal_recall 数据（range: 25.0%~76.7%），Table 7 cross-split 列显示 \"---\"。如果审稿人想看 cross-split 的 recall 分布，数据是有的。之前审查报告中已指出 caption 对 \"---\" 的解释有误（说 gen eval 不能产 recall，但 fixed-split 也是 gen eval 且有 recall）。\n\n**总结：corrected 数字没有暴露需要新实验才能堵上的缺口。** C1 不受影响，C2 实际被加强。最大的潜在 reviewer attack surface 是 (a) eval variance 差异的解释和 (b) intra-episode contamination，但两者都有防线（variance 差异 → logit smoothing effect; contamination → 加强 negative result）。如果要预防性补实验，优先级排序：death-only gen eval retrain > episode-level split ablation > cross-split int recall 补全——但全部 non-critical。",
  "project": "simgate-modular-routing"
}
```

</details>

**Old eval (old split algorithm, n_val=300)**:
- balanced_accuracy: 0.4148 (41.5%)
- internal_recall: 0.2667 (26.7%)

**Corrected eval (training split algorithm, n_val=305)**:


- balanced_accuracy: 0.753 (75.3%)
- internal_recall: 0.8333 (83.3%)
- accuracy: 0.6885

This is a massive improvement — the corrected evaluation reveals plan-004-v5 is actually performing at 75.3% balanced accuracy and 83.3% internal recall, which is substantially better than the old numbers suggested. This makes me reconsider the model's actual capabilities, especially given it was trained with Qwen3-1.7B SFT using gradient accumulation of 1 on the implicit data.

The original "negative" assessment was based on a 41.5% balanced accuracy from the generation eval, but that was measuring against a different validation split than what the model actually trained on. Now that I'm using the same split algorithm as training, the corrected eval shows 75.3% — a dramatic difference. The Trainer's own evaluation during training showed 79.1%, so there's only a 3.8 percentage point gap between training and generation eval, which is much more reasonable than the ~20pp gap I was seeing before.

But there's something important to consider: plan-004-v5 used gradient accumulation of 1, while the ablation models used different settings, so I need to account for that difference when comparing performance.

Now I'm realizing the paper's claims about gradient accumulation being critical might be based on the old, incorrect evaluation. The paper states that ga=1 fails to achieve meaningful routing discrimination with only 26.7% internal recall, while ga=8 produces non-degenerate results above 50%. But with the corrected eval, ga=1 is showing 83.3% internal recall and 75.3% balanced accuracy — which completely contradicts the paper's narrative in sections 4.3 and the router-details appendix. I need to check what the corrected eval shows for ablation-b with ga=8 to understand the full picture.

Looking at the corrected results, ablation-b with ga=8 and seed 42 gives 57.06% balanced accuracy, while plan-004-v5 with ga=1 and the same seed reaches 75.3%. That's a dramatic reversal — ga=1 is now substantially outperforming ga=8 under the corrected evaluation. Both trained with seed 42 on the same data but different ga values. The corrected eval shows ga=1 >> ga=8, which contradicts the paper's findings. But here's the issue — both models use the same data split since they both trained with seed=42, and the training code applies a 15% eval split with that seed. So they're evaluating on the same held-out validation set, which means the difference in performance should be real and not an artifact of different splits.

This is potentially critical: the paper claims ga=1 fails with 26.7% internal recall, but that was based on the wrong eval. The corrected eval shows ga=1 at 75.3% balanced accuracy versus ga=8 at 57.1%, which would flip the paper's conclusion about the ga ablation. Though I'm noticing the 75.3% seems unusually high compared to other results, so I should verify this more carefully.

Looking at the confusion matrix for ga=1, I'm seeing 185 correct detections and 25 correct internal predictions out of 305 samples, which gives those recall rates. The ga=1 model is predicting "internal" for about 38% of samples, which is worth examining more closely.

The ga=8 models show different patterns with lower internal prediction rates and more balanced tradeoffs. The key question is whether this 75.3% balanced accuracy is a genuine result or an artifact of the small validation set—with only 30 true internal cases, even small prediction shifts could swing the metric significantly. But what matters most is the discrepancy: the paper claims ga=1 fails with 26.7% internal recall, yet my corrected evaluation shows it actually achieves 83.3%.

This is a critical contradiction that needs immediate escalation. However, I'm noticing something odd—the correction dramatically improved ga=1's score from 41.5% to 75.3%, while ga=8 barely changed from 57.6% to 57.1%. This asymmetry suggests the old evaluation used a different validation split (300 vs 305 samples), and ga=1 may have simply performed better on the corrected split due to its different training dynamics, possibly because it overfits more heavily to the training distribution. to its specific training subset, and the corrected eval (which uses the matching split) captures this overfitting.

Actually, this IS a form of data leakage: the corrected eval uses the training's split function, which means the val set is exactly what the Trainer saw during evaluation. If the ga=1 model's behavior is somehow contingent on the specific val set it saw during training (e.g., through interactions between training batch composition and the val examples), the corrected eval would reflect this.

But this applies equally to ga=8 models. The ga=8 models don't show such dramatic improvement under corrected eval (57.6→57.1 for seed 42). So the asymmetric impact is interesting.

Actually, let me reconsider. The ga=1 model's old eval was on a completely different val set (300 samples with sklearn episode-level split) where it scored 41.5%. The corrected eval is on the training's own val set (305 samples with per-task step-level split) where it scores 75.3%. This massive gap reveals the ga=1 model is heavily overfit to its specific training/val split boundaries — it performs well on data it encountered during training but poorly on a different held-out set. The ga=8 models show much more robustness across eval splits (old: 57.6%, corrected: 57.1%), suggesting they're not as sensitive to which validation set is used. So the "ga=1 fails" claim in the paper was based on the wrong eval split, but the corrected eval still raises concerns about whether the 75.3% score reflects genuine routing ability or just overfitting to the particular split structure. Under CORRECTED eval (right split), ga=1 appears to succeed spectacularly. But the massive gap between old and corrected eval for ga=1 (vs minimal gap for ga=8) suggests ga=1's success is fragile and split-dependent.

This needs to be flagged as CRITICAL because:
1. A specific paper claim (main.tex:130, "with gradient accumulation of 1, the model fails to achieve meaningful routing discrimination (internal recall 26.7%)") may be wrong
2. The corrected eval shows the opposite result
3. The interpretation matters for the paper's narrative about router training

The paper's claim about ga=1 achieving only 26.7% internal recall is directly contradicted by the corrected eval showing 83.3%, which invalidates that statement. While the gradient accumulation ablation is just a hyperparameter detail in the appendix and doesn't affect the core finding about death avoidance dominance, this is still a factual error that needs correction. The bigger puzzle is why the paper uses ga=8 (57.1% balanced accuracy) when ga=1 achieves 75.3%, which suggests there might be other factors at play beyond what the metrics show.

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_013BePzX9vo2EKsxBnfu3wJX`</summary>

```text
128	We use Qwen3-1.7B for the router to avoid circular dependency with the Qwen2.5-7B data-generating model.
129	
130	Gradient accumulation is critical for the implicit router: with gradient accumulation of 1, the model fails to achieve meaningful routing discrimination (internal recall 26.7\%); with gradient accumulation of 8, the model becomes non-degenerate (internal recall above 50\% across all seeds). All main-text results use gradient accumulation 8.
131	
132	Under a fixed data split, cross-seed balanced accuracy ranges from 52.7\% to 58.1\%, but internal recall varies from 51.5\% to 81.8\%, reflecting threshold sensitivity. When the data split is also varied, balanced accuracy variance increases further (Appendix~\ref{app:seed-robustness}). Deployed end-to-end, the trained router allocates 97.0\% of episodes to deterministic computation with 17.6\% death rate, and misrouting cost (death from internally-routed episodes) exceeds the benefit of correctly routing rare internal-preferred cases.
133	
134	\paragraph{Evaluation methods.} All balanced accuracy results in the main text and Table~\ref{tab:seed-robustness} use \emph{generation-based evaluation}: the model generates a routing label as text (\texttt{enable\_thinking=False}), which is parsed for classification. This is the same pipeline used for the model-size ablation (\S\ref{sec:ablations}). We also computed \emph{Trainer evaluation} (logit-based) for comparison: under a fixed split, Trainer evaluation yields $57.6 \pm 0.5\%$ balanced accuracy versus $56.2 \pm 2.4\%$ for generation evaluation on the same seeds. The logit--generation gap arises because token-level probabilities and greedy-decoded sequences can disagree near decision boundaries; we note this as a limitation (\S\ref{sec:discussion}). The substantially lower variance under Trainer evaluation ($0.5$\,pp vs.\ $2.4$\,pp, a ${\sim}5{\times}$ difference) likely reflects the smoothing effect of soft logit-based classification, which averages over near-boundary samples that generation-based evaluation classifies discretely.
```

Comparing across the metrics, the ga=1 model's corrected evaluation is dramatically better than its old one—a jump of 33.8 percentage points in balanced accuracy and 56.6 points in recall, suggesting the original evaluation had serious issues.

The paper's claim that ga=1 fails at routing discrimination is based on that flawed old evaluation. With the corrected numbers, ga=1 actually achieves 83.3% internal recall and 75.3% balanced accuracy, which directly contradicts the paper's conclusion.

Looking at the confusion matrix for ga=1 under corrected evaluation, I'm seeing that out of 275 true detection samples, 185 are correctly classified (67.3%) while 90 get misclassified as internal (32.7%), and for the 30 true internal samples, 25 are correctly identified (83.3%). This raises the question of whether the 75.3% balanced accuracy is genuinely strong performance or if it's somehow inflated by the evaluation correction.

The model is heavily biased toward predicting internal—it labels 115 out of 305 samples as internal (37.7%) when the true rate is only 9.8%, which artificially boosts the internal recall to 83.3% while the internal precision drops to just 21.7%. The balanced accuracy of 75.3% comes from averaging the two class recalls, both above 67%, but this masks the severe class imbalance problem.

Comparing to ga=8 with the corrected evaluation, I'm seeing a balanced accuracy of 57.06% with 76.67% internal recall, so while the internal recall is similarly high, the deterministic recall is much lower, which explains the overall lower balanced accuracy.

What's striking is how differently ga=1 performs across evaluation methods—it gets 75.3% on the corrected eval but fails badly on the old eval using a different validation split. The ga=8 model is far more consistent across both evaluation approaches (57.6% vs 57.1%), which suggests it's actually generalizing better rather than memorizing the specific training-validation boundary like ga=1 appears to be doing. The paper's reported number of 26.7% from the old eval is incorrect, but the real issue is that ga=1's apparent success on the corrected eval is likely an artifact of overfitting to that particular split. under corrected eval. The claim needs revision.

Key points for the report:

2. Corrected eval shows ga=1 balanced_acc=75.3%, int_recall=83.3%
3. BUT: massive split sensitivity (old eval: 41.5%, corrected: 75.3%) suggests ga=1 is highly overfit
4. ga=8 is split-robust (old: 57.6%, corrected: 57.1%), ga=1 is not (old: 41.5%, corrected: 75.3%)
